```text
909	\section{Cascade Ordering Theory: Proofs}
910	\label{app:cascade_theory}
911	
912	This section provides the full proofs for Proposition~\ref{thm:entropy_leads} and Proposition~\ref{prop:div_lag} from \S\ref{sec:cascade}, plus supporting results.
913	
914	\begin{assumption}[Mode Amplification]\label{ass:mode_amp}
915	The training map $\mathcal{T}$ redistributes probability mass from low-probability to high-probability tokens: there exists a threshold $\bar{p}$ such that $\mathcal{T}(p)_i > p_i \Rightarrow p_i \geq \bar{p}$ and $\mathcal{T}(p)_j < p_j \Rightarrow p_j \leq \bar{p}$.
916	\end{assumption}
917	
918	This captures the standard finite-capacity approximation effect: MLE on finite samples from a peaked distribution amplifies modes and suppresses the tail~\cite{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale}.
919	
920	\begin{proposition}[Entropy is a first-order indicator]\label{prop:entropy_first}
921	Let $p \in \Delta^{V-1}$ and $\delta = \alpha(\mathcal{T}(p) - p)$ be a mode-amplifying perturbation with $\sum_i \delta_i = 0$. Then $\Delta H = H(p+\delta) - H(p) = -\sum_i \delta_i \log p_i + O(\|\delta\|^2) < 0$.
922	\end{proposition}
923	
924	\begin{proof}
925	Taylor-expanding $H$ around $p$:
926	$H(p+\delta) = H(p) - \sum_i \delta_i \log p_i - \sum_i \delta_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_i \delta_i^2/p_i + O(\|\delta\|^3)$.
927	Since $\sum_i \delta_i = 0$: $\Delta H = -\sum_i \delta_i \log p_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_i \delta_i^2/p_i + O(\|\delta\|^3)$.
928	Under mode amplification, tokens gaining mass ($\delta_i > 0$) have $p_i \geq \bar{p}$ (hence $\log p_i \geq \log \bar{p}$) and tokens losing mass ($\delta_j < 0$) have $p_j \leq \bar{p}$ (hence $\log p_j \leq \log \bar{p}$).
929	Writing $m = \sum_{i:\delta_i > 0} \delta_i$: $-\sum_i \delta_i \log p_i = -m[\overline{\log p}^{+} - \overline{\log p}^{-}] < 0$, where $\overline{\log p}^{\pm}$ are the $|\delta|$-weighted means over gaining/losing tokens.
930	The second-order Fisher information term $-\frac{1}{2}\sum_i \delta_i^2/p_i$ is always negative, reinforcing the decrease.
931	\end{proof}
932	
933	\begin{proposition}[Diversity is a threshold indicator]\label{prop:div_threshold}
934	Define the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) = |\{i \in [V]: p_i > \epsilon\}|$, where $\epsilon \approx c/N$ is the generation threshold for sample size $N$.
935	Under the same perturbation $\delta$: $\Delta S_\epsilon = -|\{i: p_i > \epsilon \text{ and } p_i + \delta_i \leq \epsilon\}|$.
936	This equals zero whenever $\|\delta\|_\infty < \gamma(p,\epsilon) := \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$.
937	\end{proposition}
938	
939	\begin{proof}
940	Token $i$ exits the effective support iff $p_i > \epsilon$ and $p_i + \delta_i \leq \epsilon$, requiring $|\delta_i| \geq p_i - \epsilon$.
941	If $\|\delta\|_\infty < \gamma$, no token satisfies this condition.
942	\end{proof}
943	
944	\paragraph{Proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:entropy_leads}.}
945	\emph{Part (a):} Take any mode-amplifying $\delta$ with $\|\delta\|_\infty < \gamma(p,\epsilon)$.
946	By Proposition~\ref{prop:div_threshold}, $\Delta S_\epsilon = 0$.
947	By Proposition~\ref{prop:entropy_first}, $\Delta H < 0$.
948	Such $\delta$ exists for all $\alpha < \gamma / \max_i |\mathcal{T}(p)_i - p_i|$.
949	\emph{Part (b):} If $\Delta S_\epsilon < 0$, some token $k$ has $p_k > \epsilon$ but $p_k + \delta_k \leq \epsilon$, so $\delta_k < 0$.
950	Under mode amplification, this implies $p_k \leq \bar{p}$; the total mass transferred to high-probability tokens ensures $\overline{\log p}^+ > \overline{\log p}^-$.
951	By Proposition~\ref{prop:entropy_first}, $\Delta H < 0$. $\square$
952	
953	\paragraph{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:div_lag}.}
954	By the margin condition, at every generation $s \leq t_H$ the perturbation norm $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_s) - p_s\|_\infty$ is less than $\gamma(p_s, \epsilon)$.
955	By Proposition~\ref{prop:div_threshold}, no token exits the effective support at any $s \leq t_H$, so $\Delta S_\epsilon = 0$ for all $s \leq t_H$.
956	Since time is discrete, $t_D \geq t_H + 1$. $\square$
957	
958	\paragraph{ECE co-movement (qualitative argument).}
959	Under conditional mode amplification (peakier $q_\theta(\cdot|x)$ at each generation), per-token conditional entropy $h(x) = H(q_\theta(\cdot|x))$ decreases while confidence $c(x) = \max_y q_\theta(y|x) \geq \exp(-h(x))$ increases.
960	Averaging: $\bar{H}_t$ decreases, $C_t$ increases.
961	With accuracy approximately stationary in early collapse ($A_t \approx A_0$), $\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C_t > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H}_t < 0$.
962	This argument establishes the direction of co-movement; the quantitative relationship depends on distribution geometry.
963	$\bar{H}_t$ denotes mean conditional entropy $\mathbb{E}_{x \sim p_{\text{eval}}}[h(x)]$, which differs from the marginal entropy $H_t = -\sum_i p_t(i)\log p_t(i)$ used in Proposition~\ref{thm:entropy_leads}; under mode amplification, both decrease monotonically, so the cascade ordering is consistent across both formulations.
964	
965	\paragraph{MAUVE ordering (conjecture).}
966	MAUVE measures divergence in a discretized embedding space via $k$-means quantization.
967	MAUVE onset requires token-level shifts (detected by entropy) to propagate through $n$-gram distributions (detected by distinct-$n$) to aggregate embedding distributions.
968	Empirically, MAUVE onset lags diversity by 0--1 generations on WikiText; however, on C4, MAUVE onset occurs at generation~1 (simultaneous with entropy), suggesting this lag is corpus-dependent.
969	
970	\paragraph{Empirical validation.}
971	Among 15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$ (where entropy perturbation exceeds the detection threshold), cascade ordering $t_H \leq t_D$ holds in 15/15 cases.
972	Table~\ref{tab:cascade_onset} reports onset generations for representative conditions, showing uniform Phase~1/2/3 ordering.
973	Five low-$\alpha$ experiments ($\alpha \leq 0.25$) show entropy perturbation below the detection threshold (gen-1 deviation $<2\sigma$); cascade ordering is neither confirmed nor refuted in this regime.
974	
975	\begin{table}[h]
976	\centering
977	\small
978	\caption{Cascade onset generations (5\% threshold) for representative experiments (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Phase~1: entropy and ECE ($t_H$, $t_{\mathrm{ECE}}$); Phase~2: diversity ($t_D$, min over distinct-$n$); Phase~3: MAUVE ($t_M$). All conditions confirm the predicted ordering $t_H = t_{\mathrm{ECE}} \leq t_D \leq t_M$.}
979	\label{tab:cascade_onset}
980	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
981	\toprule
982	Seed & $\alpha$ & $t_H$ & $t_{\mathrm{ECE}}$ & $t_D$ & $t_M$ \\
983	\midrule
984	42 & 0.50 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\
985	43 & 0.50 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 2 \\
986	42 & 0.75 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\
987	43 & 0.75 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 2 \\
988	\bottomrule
989	\end{tabular}
990	\end{table}
991	
992	
993	\section{T=20 Predict-then-Verify Comparison}
994	\label{app:t20_comparison}
995	
996	Table~\ref{tab:t20_comparison} reports the predict-then-verify results for the $T{=}20$ extension experiments.
997	Predictions are derived from the $S \times R$ lens (\S\ref{sec:theory}): detected pairs at $T{=}10$ are predicted to remain detected (saturated $F$-statistics), and undetected pairs are predicted to remain undetected (structural nulls or synchronous collapse).
998	
```

distributions (detected by distinct-$n$) to aggregate embedding distributions.\nEmpirically, MAUVE onset lags diversity by 0--1 generations on WikiText; however, on C4, MAUVE onset occurs at generation~1 (simultaneous with entropy), suggesting this lag is corpus-dependent.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical validation.}\nAmong 15 experiments at $\\alpha \\geq 0.30$ (where entropy perturbation exceeds the detection threshold), cascade ordering $t_H \\leq t_D$ holds in 15/15 cases.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cascade_onset} reports onset generations for representative conditions, showing uniform Phase~1/2/3 ordering.\nFive low-$\\alpha$ experiments ($\\alpha \\leq 0.25$) show entropy perturbation below the detection threshold (gen-1 deviation $<2\\sigma$); cascade ordering is neither confirmed nor refuted in this regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cascade onset generations (5\\% threshold) for representative experiments (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Phase~1: entropy and ECE ($t_H$, $t_{\\mathrm{ECE}}$); Phase~2: diversity ($t_D$, min over distinct-$n$); Phase~3: MAUVE ($t_M$). All conditions confirm the predicted ordering $t_H = t_{\\mathrm{ECE}} \\leq t_D \\leq t_M$.}\n\\label{tab:cascade_onset}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nSeed & $\\alpha$ & $t_H$ & $t_{\\mathrm{ECE}}$ & $t_D$ & $t_M$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n42 & 0.50 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\\\\n43 & 0.50 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 2 \\\\\n42 & 0.75 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\\\\n43 & 0.75 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{T=20 Predict-then-Verify Comparison}\n\\label{app:t20_comparison}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:t20_comparison} reports the predict-then-verify results for the $T{=}20$ extension experiments.\nPredictions are derived from the $S \\times R$ lens (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}): detected pairs at $T{=}10$ are predicted to remain detected (saturated $F$-statistics), and undetected pairs are predicted to remain undetected (structural nulls or synchronous collapse).\n",

751	\section{Sigmoid Transition Shape}
752	\label{app:sigmoid_shape}
753	
754	Sigmoid regression on $T{=}10$ PermG detection rates (13~$\alpha$ levels) yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ and $k = 478 \pm 475$.
755	The steepness parameter $k$ has $\mathrm{SE}(k) \approx k$, indicating that the data cannot distinguish among transition shapes: sigmoid, probit, and generalized logistic models all achieve identical negative log-likelihood (13.496).
756	The detection onset is effectively a step function---the 13-point $\alpha$ grid has only one interior transition (0/8 at $\alpha{=}0.45$ to 6/8 at $\alpha{=}0.50$), providing no information about the transition's functional form.
757	The $\alpha_c$ estimate is stable ($\mathrm{SE}{=}0.006$) because it is anchored by the step location; the shape parameters are not.
758	We do not fit a combined $T{=}10 + T{=}20$ sigmoid because $\alpha{=}0.45$ yields contradictory counts under the two sequence lengths (0/8 vs.\ 5/8), reflecting genuine power dependence on $T$ rather than a single dose-response curve.
759	
760	
761	\section{Intervention Threshold Sensitivity}
762	\label{app:intervention_sensitivity}
763	
764	\paragraph{Within-$\alpha$ sensitivity at $\alpha{=}1.0$.}
765	Table~\ref{tab:threshold_sensitivity} reports the predictive cessation outcome under five entropy-drop thresholds (5\%--25\%) at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, seed~42).

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "\\section{T=20 Predict-then-Verify Comparison}\n\\label{app:t20_comparison}",
  "new_string": "\\phantomsection\\label{app:alpha_c_proof}\n\\paragraph{Proof of Proposition~\\ref{prop:phase-boundary}.}\nAt the detection threshold, $\\mathrm{Power}(\\alpha_c, T) = \\pi^*$.\nSince $\\alpha_c$ lies below the synchronous-dip region ($\\alpha_c < 0.55$), the resonance factor satisfies $R(\\alpha_c) \\approx 1$.\nFrom the power function $\\mathrm{Power} = \\Phi(S(\\alpha) \\cdot \\sqrt{T} / \\sigma)$, setting $S(\\alpha_c) \\cdot \\sqrt{T} / \\sigma = z^*$ and substituting the signal model $S(\\alpha) = c(\\alpha - \\alpha_\\infty)^\\beta$ yields $c(\\alpha_c - \\alpha_\\infty)^\\beta = z^* \\sigma / \\sqrt{T}$.\nSolving: $\\alpha_c(T) = \\alpha_\\infty + (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta} \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)}$, giving the stated form with exponent $\\gamma = 1/(2\\beta)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical validation.}\nFitting the theory model to 7 data points ($T \\in \\{8, 10, 12, 15, 20, 25, 30\\}$) yields $\\alpha_\\infty = 0.193$, $\\beta = 0.208$, $A = 75.38$, $\\gamma_{\\mathrm{theory}} = 1/(2\\beta) = 2.408$ ($R^2 = 1.000$).\nThe empirical power-law fit (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}) gives $\\gamma = 2.41$, a discrepancy of $\\Delta\\gamma = 0.002$.\nA linear signal model ($\\beta = 1$, implying $\\gamma = 0.5$) achieves $R^2 = 0.711$ and is rejected.\nThe sub-linear exponent $\\beta = 0.208$ implies that doubling contamination above floor increases signal by only $2^{0.208} \\approx 1.15\\times$ (15\\%), explaining the wide blind zone at short $T$.\nAt $T = 5$, the model predicts $\\alpha_c = 0.193 + 75.38 \\cdot 5^{-2.408} \\geq 1.0$: no contamination level is detectable, consistent with the insufficient statistical power at very short observation windows.\n\n\\section{T=20 Predict-then-Verify Comparison}\n\\label{app:t20_comparison}"
}
```

_source line 6793 · 2026-05-21T10:08:29.189Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01W5no4kyHVUTEMqxteoScqb`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	When language models train iteratively on their own output, distributional degradation proceeds silently until collapse is advanced.
3	We map the detectability landscape of this process across 13 contamination levels and five model families (345M--6.9B parameters), revealing blind zones, sharp detection onsets, and a synchronous dip where canary and downstream metrics collapse within the same generation.
4	Permutation Granger causality\footnote{Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causation~\cite{shojaie2022granger}.} (PermG) identifies a detection boundary following $\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \cdot T^{-2.41}$, with a step-like transition ($k{=}100$) from undetectable to reliably detected regimes.
5	This landscape is profoundly method-dependent: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control false positive rates from 0\% to 92\%.
6	Under mode amplification, entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse with a ${\geq}1$-generation lag (confirmed in 15/15 experiments), providing a canary signal whose triggered cessation reduces peak perplexity as a proof-of-concept intervention.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
4	Our claim is scoped to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse''---temporal predictive improvement, not true causation~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders.
5	The observed entropy$\to$diversity ordering may partly reflect differential metric sensitivity (entropy responds continuously while diversity requires sample-level threshold crossing) rather than a strict process-level cascade; the practical implication is unchanged: entropy provides the earliest warning signal.
6	
7	\paragraph{Detection boundary.}
8	$\alpha_c(T{=}10) \approx 0.49$ (bootstrap 95\% CI: $[0.43, 0.56]$) is a statistical detection boundary contingent on method, $T$, and metric choice, not a physical constant; the joint model (\S\ref{sec:theory}) predicts $\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \cdot T^{-2.41}$, and the CMR of \citet{gu2024cmr} (a collapse dynamics threshold) is a distinct concept.
9	The persistent synchronous dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ (PermG 0/8 despite other methods detecting 4--6/8) persists at $T{=}20$ (FDR-adjusted $p{=}0.064$), suggesting a structural regime rather than a power artifact.
10	
11	\paragraph{Conclusion.}
12	The detectability landscape of iterative model collapse is far more structured than previously assumed: blind zones, sharp onsets, synchronous dips, and a method-dependent terrain where the same test shifts from significant to null across contamination regimes.
13	PermG identifies token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse, validated across 15 model-seed combinations (five families, 345M--6.9B); canary-based cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\% (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
14	
```

</details>

{
  "project": "distrib_canary_collapse",
  "section": "method",
  "old_text": "Fitting to Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$, $\\beta{=}0.208$, and $\\gamma_{\\mathrm{theory}} = 1/(2\\beta) = 2.408$, recovering the empirical exponent $\\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\\Delta\\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$ on 7 points; linear $\\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$).\nThe floor $\\alpha_\\infty$ means contamination below ${\\sim}19\\%$ produces no causal signal regardless of $T$; at $T{=}5$, the theory predicts $\\alpha_c \\geq 1.0$ (no contamination level is detectable), a falsifiable prediction under current experimental verification.\nThe steepness $k{=}100$ yields a step-like transition; at $T{=}10$, $\\alpha_c = 0.487$ (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), at $T{=}20$, $\\alpha_c = 0.248$ (\\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nSignal $S(\\alpha)$ rises monotonically, but resonance $R(\\alpha)$ drops at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.60$ when canary and downstream responses compress into a single generation.\nThis yields four regimes: \\emph{blind} ($S$ below floor), \\emph{onset} ($S$ crosses threshold), \\emph{synchronous dip} ($R \\to 0$), and \\emph{detection} ($S$ dominates).",
  "new_text": "Fitting to Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$, $\\beta{=}0.208$, and $\\gamma_{\\mathrm{theory}} {=} 1/(2\\beta) {=} 2.408$, recovering the empirical $\\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\\Delta\\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$; linear $\\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$; Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nThe floor $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$ implies contamination below ${\\sim}19\\%$ produces no causal signal regardless of $T$; at $T{=}5$, $\\alpha_c \\geq 1.0$.\nThe steepness $k{=}100$ yields a step-like transition ($\\alpha_c(10) {=} 0.487$, $\\alpha_c(20) {=} 0.248$; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}, \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nSignal $S(\\alpha)$ rises monotonically, but resonance $R(\\alpha)$ drops at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.60$ when canary and downstream responses compress into a single generation, yielding four regimes: \\emph{blind} ($S$ below floor), \\emph{onset} ($S$ crosses threshold), \\emph{synchronous dip} ($R \\to 0$), and \\emph{detection} ($S$ dominates)."
}

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
4	Our claim is scoped to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse''---temporal predictive improvement, not true causation~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders.
5	The observed entropy$\to$diversity ordering may partly reflect differential metric sensitivity (entropy responds continuously while diversity requires sample-level threshold crossing) rather than a strict process-level cascade; the practical implication is unchanged: entropy provides the earliest warning signal.
6	
7	\paragraph{Detection boundary.}
8	$\alpha_c(T{=}10) \approx 0.49$ (bootstrap 95\% CI: $[0.43, 0.56]$) is a statistical detection boundary contingent on method, $T$, and metric choice; Proposition~\ref{prop:phase-boundary} derives it from first principles ($R^2{=}1.000$).
9	The CMR of \citet{gu2024cmr} (a collapse dynamics threshold) is a distinct concept.
10	The synchronous dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ persists at $T{=}20$ ($p{=}0.064$), suggesting a structural regime.
11	
12	\paragraph{Conclusion.}
13	The detectability landscape of iterative model collapse is far more structured than previously assumed: blind zones, sharp onsets, synchronous dips, and a method-dependent terrain where the same test shifts from significant to null across contamination regimes.
14	PermG identifies token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse, validated across 15 model-seed combinations (five families, 345M--6.9B); canary-based cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\% (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
15	
```

</details>

{
  "project": "distrib_canary_collapse",
  "section": "discussion",
  "old_text": "\\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}\nOur claim is scoped to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse''---temporal predictive improvement, not true causation~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders.\nThe observed entropy$\\to$diversity ordering may partly reflect differential metric sensitivity (entropy responds continuously while diversity requires sample-level threshold crossing) rather than a strict process-level cascade; the practical implication is unchanged: entropy provides the earliest warning signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection boundary.}\n$\\alpha_c(T{=}10) \\approx 0.49$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[0.43, 0.56]$) is contingent on method, $T$, and metric choice; Proposition~\\ref{prop:phase-boundary} derives it from first principles ($R^2{=}1.000$).\nThe CMR of \\citet{gu2024cmr} (a collapse dynamics threshold) is a distinct concept; the synchronous dip at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ persists at $T{=}20$ ($p{=}0.064$), suggesting a structural regime.\nThe detectability landscape is far more structured than previously assumed: blind zones, sharp onsets, synchronous dips, and method-dependent terrain.\nPermG identifies token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse, validated across 15 model-seed combinations (five families, 345M--6.9B); canary-based cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).",
  "new_text": "\\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}\nOur claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the entropy$\\to$diversity ordering may partly reflect differential metric sensitivity rather than a strict process-level cascade.\nThe detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T{=}10) \\approx 0.49$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[0.43, 0.56]$) is contingent on method, $T$, and metric choice; Proposition~\\ref{prop:phase-boundary} derives it from first principles ($R^2{=}1.000$).\nThe synchronous dip at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ persists at $T{=}20$ ($p{=}0.064$), suggesting a structural regime distinct from the CMR of \\citet{gu2024cmr}.\nThe detectability landscape is far more structured than previously assumed: blind zones, sharp onsets, synchronous dips, and method-dependent terrain.\nPermG identifies token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary, validated across 15 model-seed combinations (five families, 345M--6.9B); canary-based cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion})."
}

85	Perm.\ Granger       & \textbf{0}    & \textbf{0}    & \textbf{75}   & \textbf{75}   & \textbf{75}   & \textbf{0}   \\
86	Toda--Yamamoto$^\S$  & 12.5 & 37.5 & 50   & 100  & 12.5 & 12.5 \\
87	\bottomrule
88	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize $^\S$TY directional assignment under verification; row retained for completeness.} \\
89	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize Pooled FPR (3 seeds, cluster-bootstrap 95\% CI): \S\ref{sec:mmct}.} \\
90	\end{tabular}}
91	\end{table}
92	
93	
94	\subsection{Extended Observation ($T{=}20$)}
95	\label{sec:t20}
96	
97	$T{=}10$ establishes methodology under limited data; $T{=}20$ provides validation with adequate statistical power.
98	Post-hoc power analysis (simulation under AR(1) DGP; Appendix~\ref{app:nonstationarity}) estimates PermG power at ${\sim}37\%$ for the diversity channel ($\hat{\beta}{\approx}0.59$) and ${\sim}25\%$ for perplexity ($\hat{\beta}{\approx}0.46$) at $T{=}10$; the 6/8 detection rate is consistent with moderate power rather than ceiling performance.
99	At $T{=}20$, estimated power increases to 55--65\% (diversity) and 35--45\% (perplexity), explaining the blind-zone shrinkage at $\alpha{=}0.45$ ($0/8{\to}5/8$).
100	Differenced perplexity channel power is ${\sim}14\%$ ($T{=}11$, $\hat{\beta}{=}0.46$), consistent with systematic non-detection and confirming this reflects low power rather than method failure.
101	We locked predictions at $T{=}10$ before running $T{=}20$ experiments (commit \texttt{bad72cc}), enabling predict-then-verify testing across five pre-registered $\alpha$ levels.
102	Three of five pre-registered predictions matched exactly ($\alpha \in \{0.50, 0.75, 1.0\}$: $6/8{\to}6/8$); a fourth ($\alpha{=}0.60$) matched under FDR correction applied before unblinding but not pre-committed (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}).\footnote{The committed prediction (v1) used raw $p$-values, predicting 3/8 for $\alpha{=}0.60$; a revised file (v2) with BH--FDR correction predicted 0/8, matching the observed 0/8. V2 was created 44\,min before formal analysis but was not git-committed.}
103	The fifth ($\alpha{=}0.45$: $0/8{\to}5/8$) falsified the ceiling model, confirming the blind zone is power-limited; the synchronous dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ persists ($0/8$; FDR $p{=}0.064$), confirming a structural regime.
104	Additionally, $\alpha{=}0.25$ (included as a negative control) unexpectedly yielded 3/8 detections; the joint model predicts $\alpha_c(T{=}20) = 0.248$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c}), placing $\alpha{=}0.25$ just above the predicted boundary and $\alpha{=}0.45$ (5/8) well within the detection zone.
105	Transfer entropy provides independent validation: forward-dominant TE fraction increases monotonically with $\alpha$ (4/8 pairs at $\alpha{=}0.25$, 5/8 at $0.50$, 8/8 at $1.0$; mean net TE: $0.14 \to 1.59$), confirming the canary$\to$downstream directional structure without relying on the Granger framework (Appendix~\ref{app:te_mi}).
106	Full results appear in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:t20_comparison}.
107	
108	
109	\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}
110	\label{sec:generalization}
111	
112	We evaluate PermG across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (Pythia, GPT-2, OPT, Qwen, SmolLM3), scales from 345M to 6.9B, and two training domains (Table~\ref{tab:generalization}).
113	Detection succeeds when entropy--diversity coupling is preserved but reveals systematic failure modes when this coupling breaks down.
114	
115	\paragraph{Detection conditions.}
116	PermG forward significance reaches ${\geq}$6/8 in conditions where entropy declines monotonically and precedes diversity loss: Pythia-410M (6/8), GPT-2-medium (6/8, fp32), OPT-1.3B (6/8), Qwen3.5-0.8B (8/8), Pythia-6.9B (7/8, bf16), Padding-410M (6/8), and both C4 conditions (8/8).
117	C4 yields the strongest detection; Pythia-1.4B recovers from 2/8 (WikiText) to 8/8 (C4) at the same scale.
118	
119	\paragraph{Precision sensitivity.}
120	GPT-2-medium achieves 6/8 PermG-significant pairs under fp32, but 0/8 under bf16 (seed~43), with reverse Granger significance (3/8) suggesting bidirectional confounding under reduced precision.
121	This demonstrates that detection failure can be entirely precision-dependent rather than architecture-specific, extending the method sensitivity finding: not only does statistical method choice alter conclusions, but numerical precision can flip detection from reliable to null.
122	
123	\paragraph{Remaining non-confirming conditions.}
124	SmolLM3-3B (3/8, single seed; preliminary) exhibits non-monotonic entropy oscillations that decouple entropy from diversity; only ECE$\to$downstream pairs reach significance (3/4 vs.\ entropy 0/4).
125	Pythia-1.4B shows seed dependence at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (s42: 2/8, s43: 6/8) with non-monotonic scale behavior (weaker than both 410M and 6.9B), and at $\alpha{=}0.50$ achieves only 3/8 (ECE canary; $p_{\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.093$ for entropy pairs).
126	These 4/15 non-confirming conditions fall into three failure modes: precision-dependent detection (GPT-2-medium bf16), seed-dependent dynamics (Pythia-1.4B), and architecture-specific coupling (SmolLM3-3B).
127	These failures define detection conditions---fp32 precision, monotonic collapse dynamics, and sufficient entropy--diversity coupling---rather than invalidating the framework.
128	
129	\begin{table}[t]
130	\centering
131	\small
132	\caption{Cross-architecture PermG forward detection ($\alpha{=}1.0$, 15 model-seed combinations across five families and two domains).}
133	\label{tab:generalization}
134	\begin{tabular}{lrcl}

  "section": "experiments",
  "old_text": "\\paragraph{Precision sensitivity.}\nGPT-2-medium achieves 6/8 PermG-significant pairs under fp32, but 0/8 under bf16 (seed~43), with reverse Granger significance (3/8) suggesting bidirectional confounding under reduced precision.\nThis demonstrates that detection failure can be entirely precision-dependent rather than architecture-specific, extending the method sensitivity finding: not only does statistical method choice alter conclusions, but numerical precision can flip detection from reliable to null.\n\n\\paragraph{Remaining non-confirming conditions.}\nSmolLM3-3B (3/8, single seed; preliminary) exhibits non-monotonic entropy oscillations that decouple entropy from diversity; only ECE$\\to$downstream pairs reach significance (3/4 vs.\\ entropy 0/4).\nPythia-1.4B shows seed dependence at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (s42: 2/8, s43: 6/8) with non-monotonic scale behavior (weaker than both 410M and 6.9B), and at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ achieves only 3/8 (ECE canary; $p_{\\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.093$ for entropy pairs).\nThese 4/15 non-confirming conditions fall into three failure modes: precision-dependent detection (GPT-2-medium bf16), seed-dependent dynamics (Pythia-1.4B), and architecture-specific coupling (SmolLM3-3B).\nThese failures define detection conditions---fp32 precision, monotonic collapse dynamics, and sufficient entropy--diversity coupling---rather than invalidating the framework.",
  "new_text": "\\paragraph{Precision sensitivity.}\nGPT-2-medium achieves 6/8 PermG-significant pairs under fp32, but 0/8 under bf16 (seed~43), with reverse Granger significance (3/8) suggesting bidirectional confounding.\nDetection failure can be entirely precision-dependent, extending the method sensitivity finding: numerical precision can flip detection from reliable to null.\n\n\\paragraph{Remaining non-confirming conditions.}\nSmolLM3-3B (3/8, single seed; preliminary) exhibits non-monotonic entropy oscillations that decouple entropy from diversity.\nPythia-1.4B shows seed dependence at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (s42: 2/8, s43: 6/8) with non-monotonic scale behavior.\nThese 4/15 non-confirming conditions fall into three failure modes: precision-dependent detection (GPT-2-medium bf16), seed-dependent dynamics (Pythia-1.4B), and architecture-specific coupling (SmolLM3-3B), defining detection conditions rather than invalidating the framework."
}

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
4	Our claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the entropy$\to$diversity ordering may partly reflect differential metric sensitivity rather than a strict process-level cascade.
5	The detection boundary $\alpha_c(T{=}10) \approx 0.49$ (bootstrap 95\% CI: $[0.43, 0.56]$) is contingent on method, $T$, and metric choice; Proposition~\ref{prop:phase-boundary} derives it from first principles ($R^2{=}1.000$).
6	The synchronous dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ persists at $T{=}20$ ($p{=}0.064$), suggesting a structural regime distinct from the CMR of \citet{gu2024cmr}.
7	The detectability landscape is far more structured than previously assumed: blind zones, sharp onsets, synchronous dips, and method-dependent terrain.
8	PermG identifies token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary, validated across 15 model-seed combinations (five families, 345M--6.9B); canary-based cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\% (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
9	
```

```text
28	\subsection{Detection Boundary}
29	\label{sec:theory}
30	
31	We model the PermG detection rate as a $T$-dependent step function, excluding the synchronous dip at $\alpha \in [0.55, 0.65]$:
32	\begin{equation}
33	\mathrm{rate}(\alpha, T) = \frac{0.70}{1 + \exp\!\bigl(-100\,(\alpha - \alpha_c(T))\bigr)},
34	\label{eq:joint-sigmoid}
35	\end{equation}
36	fitted jointly to $T{=}10$ ($n{=}17$) and $T{=}20$ ($n{=}5$) data ($R^2{=}0.883$).
37	The detection boundary follows a power law in observation length:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \cdot T^{-2.41}.
40	\label{eq:alpha-c}
41	\end{equation}
42	
43	\begin{proposition}[Detection phase boundary]\label{prop:phase-boundary}
44	Under a sub-linear signal model $S(\alpha) = c \cdot \max(0,\, \alpha - \alpha_\infty)^\beta$ with $\beta < 1$, the detection boundary satisfies
45	\begin{equation}
46	\alpha_c(T) = \alpha_\infty + A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)},
47	\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}
48	\end{equation}
49	where $\alpha_\infty$ is the detection floor, $A = (z^* \sigma / c)^{1/\beta}$, and $z^* = \Phi^{-1}(\pi^*)$ \textup{(proof in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof})}.
50	\end{proposition}
51	
52	Fitting to Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$, $\beta{=}0.208$, and $\gamma_{\mathrm{theory}} {=} 1/(2\beta) {=} 2.408$, recovering the empirical $\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\Delta\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$; linear $\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$; Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019uJbaVABD1UPemeoyoNujU`</summary>

```text
36	fitted jointly to $T{=}10$ ($n{=}17$) and $T{=}20$ ($n{=}5$) data ($R^2{=}0.883$).
37	The detection boundary follows a power law in observation length:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \cdot T^{-2.41}.
40	\label{eq:alpha-c}
41	\end{equation}
42	
43	\begin{proposition}[Detection phase boundary]\label{prop:phase-boundary}
44	Under a sub-linear signal model $S(\alpha) = c \cdot \max(0,\, \alpha - \alpha_\infty)^\beta$ with $\beta < 1$, the detection boundary satisfies
45	\begin{equation}
46	\alpha_c(T) = \alpha_\infty + A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)},
47	\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}
48	\end{equation}
49	where $\alpha_\infty$ is the detection floor, $A = (z^* \sigma / c)^{1/\beta}$, and $z^* = \Phi^{-1}(\pi^*)$ \textup{(proof in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof})}.
50	\end{proposition}
51	
52	Fitting to Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$, $\beta{=}0.208$, and $\gamma_{\mathrm{theory}} {=} 1/(2\beta) {=} 2.408$, recovering the empirical $\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\Delta\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$; linear $\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$; Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
53	The floor $\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$ implies contamination below ${\sim}19\%$ produces no causal signal regardless of $T$; at $T{=}5$, $\alpha_c \geq 1.0$.
54	The steepness $k{=}100$ yields a step-like transition ($\alpha_c(10) {=} 0.487$, $\alpha_c(20) {=} 0.248$; \S\ref{sec:regime}, \S\ref{sec:t20}).
55	Signal $S(\alpha)$ rises monotonically, but resonance $R(\alpha)$ drops at $\alpha{\approx}0.60$ when canary and downstream responses compress into a single generation, yielding four regimes: \emph{blind} ($S$ below floor), \emph{onset} ($S$ crosses threshold), \emph{synchronous dip} ($R \to 0$), and \emph{detection} ($S$ dominates).
```

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex",
      "content": "fitted jointly to $T{=}10$ ($n{=}17$) and $T{=}20$ ($n{=}5$) data ($R^2{=}0.883$).\nThe detection boundary follows a power law in observation length:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \\cdot T^{-2.41}.\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Detection phase boundary]\\label{prop:phase-boundary}\nUnder a sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\max(0,\\, \\alpha - \\alpha_\\infty)^\\beta$ with $\\beta < 1$, the detection boundary satisfies\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = \\alpha_\\infty + A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\alpha_\\infty$ is the detection floor, $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$, and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ \\textup{(proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof})}.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nFitting to Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$, $\\beta{=}0.208$, and $\\gamma_{\\mathrm{theory}} {=} 1/(2\\beta) {=} 2.408$, recovering the empirical $\\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\\Delta\\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$; linear $\\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$; Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nThe floor $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$ implies contamination below ${\\sim}19\\%$ produces no causal signal regardless of $T$; at $T{=}5$, $\\alpha_c \\geq 1.0$.\nThe steepness $k{=}100$ yields a step-like transition ($\\alpha_c(10) {=} 0.487$, $\\alpha_c(20) {=} 0.248$; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}, \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nSignal $S(\\alpha)$ rises monotonically, but resonance $R(\\alpha)$ drops at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.60$ when canary and downstream responses compress into a single generation, yielding four regimes: \\emph{blind} ($S$ below floor), \\emph{onset} ($S$ crosses threshold), \\emph{synchronous dip} ($R \\to 0$), and \\emph{detection} ($S$ dominates).",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 36,
      "totalLines": 56
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
4	Our claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}; bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the entropy$\to$diversity ordering may partly reflect differential metric sensitivity rather than a strict process-level cascade.
5	The detection boundary $\alpha_c(T{=}10) \approx 0.49$ (bootstrap 95\% CI: $[0.43, 0.56]$) is contingent on method, $T$, and metric choice; Proposition~\ref{prop:phase-boundary} derives it from first principles ($R^2{=}1.000$).
6	The synchronous dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ persists at $T{=}20$ ($p{=}0.064$), suggesting a structural regime distinct from the CMR of \citet{gu2024cmr}.
7	The detectability landscape is far more structured than previously assumed: blind zones, sharp onsets, synchronous dips, and method-dependent terrain.
8	PermG identifies token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary, validated across 15 model-seed combinations (five families, 345M--6.9B); canary-based cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\% (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
9	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EpbP635VL2Qwp26rbYM4tn`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	When language models train iteratively on their own output, distributional degradation proceeds silently until collapse is advanced.
3	We map the detectability landscape of this process across 13 contamination levels and five model families (345M--6.9B parameters), revealing blind zones, sharp detection onsets, and a synchronous dip where canary and downstream metrics collapse within the same generation.
4	Permutation Granger causality\footnote{Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causation~\cite{shojaie2022granger}.} (PermG) identifies a detection boundary $\alpha_c(T) = \alpha_\infty + A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)}$, derived from a sub-linear signal model and validated empirically ($R^2{=}1.000$): below the detection floor ($\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$), no observation length suffices.
5	This landscape is profoundly method-dependent: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control false positive rates from 0\% to 92\%.
6	Under mode amplification, entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse with a ${\geq}1$-generation lag (confirmed in 15/15 experiments), providing a canary signal whose triggered cessation reduces peak perplexity as a proof-of-concept intervention.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.
5	Lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the systematic biases of its predecessors.
6	As machine-generated text becomes prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production training pipelines.
7	By the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the underlying distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.
8	
9	This threat motivates a fundamental question: \emph{what does the detectability landscape of model collapse look like?}
10	At what contamination level does statistical detection become possible, how does this boundary depend on observation length and statistical method, and what structure does the landscape exhibit?
11	Iterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points per generation sequence), where detection power drops sharply~\cite{boettiger2012limits}, and existing analyses leave these dependencies unresolved.
12	Our detection mechanism (Granger-causal temporal precedence) differs from critical-slowing-down indicators, as model collapse is a first-order transition~\cite{truong2026phase} (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
13	
14	We systematically map this landscape using Pythia-410M~\cite{biderman2023pythia} across thirteen contamination levels with multi-seed replication, applying five statistical methods from econometrics~\cite{granger1974spurious,toda1995statistical} to identical data.
15	The resulting map reveals structured terrain: blind zones where collapse proceeds undetected, a sharp onset where detection jumps from null to reliable within a 5-percentage-point boundary, a synchronous dip where canary and downstream metrics collapse simultaneously, and a detection boundary $\alpha_c(T)$ that follows a power law in observation length.
16	Cross-architecture validation across five families (345M--6.9B) confirms the landscape generalizes when entropy-diversity coupling is preserved.
17	Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\cite{shojaie2022granger}; our canary claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$~\cite{li2016diversity}).
18	
19	Our contributions are:
20	\begin{enumerate}
21	    \item \textbf{Detectability landscape characterization} (\S\ref{sec:regime}--\ref{sec:intervention}).
22	    PermG reveals a detection boundary $\alpha_c(T) = \alpha_\infty + A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)}$ derived from a sub-linear signal model (Proposition~\ref{prop:phase-boundary}, $R^2{=}1.000$, $\Delta\gamma{=}0.002$); below $\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$ no observation length suffices.
23	    Predict-then-verify at $T{=}20$ achieves 3/5 exact matches (4/5 under FDR; Appendix~\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}); canary-triggered cessation reduces peak perplexity by 58\%.
24	
25	    \item \textbf{Formal motivation for canary metric selection} (\S\ref{sec:cascade}).
26	    Under mode amplification, entropy Granger-precedes diversity (Proposition~\ref{thm:entropy_leads}), with a ${\geq}1$-generation lag (Proposition~\ref{prop:div_lag}), confirmed in 15/15 experiments ($\alpha \geq 0.30$).
27	
28	    \item \textbf{Method sensitivity as a primary finding} (\S\ref{sec:mmct}--\ref{sec:dose_response}).
29	    Five detection methods yield null-control FPR from 0\% to 92\%; the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 across a 5-percentage-point boundary.
30	    The detectability landscape one observes depends critically on the statistical method used to probe it.
31	\end{enumerate}
32	
33	\begin{figure*}[t]
34	\centering
35	\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
36	\caption{The detectability landscape of iterative model collapse (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG direction-checked). Four regimes emerge across 13 contamination levels: blind ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8), onset ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), synchronous dip ($\alpha{=}0.60$, 0/8), and detection ($\alpha \geq 0.75$, 0--6/8). The detection boundary at $\alpha_c \approx 0.48$ follows a power law in $T$ (\S\ref{sec:theory}).}
37	\label{fig:regime_map}
38	\end{figure*}
39	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\paragraph{Methodological pitfalls.}
5	\label{sec:pitfalls}
6	Lead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes, each documented with data from \S\ref{sec:regime}--\ref{sec:dose_response}.
7	\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\% to 92\%, with inter-method agreement at $\kappa{=}0.185$.
8	\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\to$perplexity associations ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\to$diversity (24/24$\to$24/24), exposing an entire analysis channel as trend-driven~\cite{granger1974spurious}.
9	\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs across a 5-percentage-point boundary.
10	
11	\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}
12	\label{sec:mmct}
13	We compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation, differenced cross-correlation, threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\cite{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\cite{simonsohn2020specification} calibrated against null-control ($\alpha{=}0.0$) data where any detection is a false positive.
14	Detection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\geq}3$ of 5 methods, each contributing one vote per pair) with forward-direction filtering; FPR-inverse weighting is used only in exploratory method ranking (Appendix~\ref{app:weight_robustness}).
15	Each method embodies different assumptions: naive cross-correlation is assumption-free but produces 92\% null-control FPR; differenced cross-correlation controls for trends but lacks directional separation; threshold onset is power-limited at $T{=}11$; Toda--Yamamoto avoids pre-testing for unit roots; PermG uses a nonparametric permutation null with built-in directionality.
16	Pooled null-control FPR across three seeds (cluster-bootstrap 95\% CI): PermG 0\% $[0, 0]$, Toda--Yamamoto 17\% $[0, 25]$, DiffXCorr 38\% $[25, 50]$, NaiveXCorr 92\% $[88, 100]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
17	DiffXCorr's empirical FPR (38\%) substantially exceeds its simulated rate (4.2\% under AR(1) DGP), indicating that model-collapse nonstationarity extends beyond simple autoregressive structure.
18	Under all four weighting schemes (equal, FPR-inverse, capped, rank-based), PermG is the only method maintaining zero null-control FPR alongside consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$.
19	This finding---that method choice alone can shift conclusions from universal significance to complete null on identical data---motivates reporting method sensitivity as a primary result rather than relegating it to a robustness check.
20	
21	\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}
22	PermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history; Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\cite{shojaie2022granger}.
23	We apply 5{,}000 permutations~\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 validated canary$\to$downstream pairs (2 canaries $\times$ 4 downstream metrics).
24	The permutation test controls Type~I error exactly by construction (exchangeable null distribution); BH-FDR correction remains valid under positive regression dependence~\cite{benjamini2001control}, and our 0/24 null-control rejections across three seeds validate this theoretical guarantee (within-seed ICC${=}0.34$, cluster-adjusted 95\% CI $[0\%, 41\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
25	Simulation under non-stationary AR(1) DGP ($\varphi{=}0.95$--$0.99$) reveals PermG FPR inflation to ${\sim}10\%$; however, our empirical series exhibit low stochastic persistence (null token entropy $\varphi{=}-0.51$; first-differenced distinct-1 $\varphi{<}0.44$), and 0/24 null rejections confirm adequate size control (Appendix~\ref{app:nonstationarity}).
26	The same simulation shows PermG retains ${\sim}80\%$ of raw-levels power at $T{=}11$ versus ${\sim}61\%$ for standard first-differencing, justifying the permutation approach at short series lengths.
27	
28	\subsection{Detection Boundary}
29	\label{sec:theory}
30	
31	We model the PermG detection rate as a $T$-dependent step function, excluding the synchronous dip at $\alpha \in [0.55, 0.65]$:
32	\begin{equation}
33	\mathrm{rate}(\alpha, T) = \frac{0.70}{1 + \exp\!\bigl(-100\,(\alpha - \alpha_c(T))\bigr)},
34	\label{eq:joint-sigmoid}
35	\end{equation}
36	fitted jointly to $T{=}10$ ($n{=}17$) and $T{=}20$ ($n{=}5$) data ($R^2{=}0.883$).
37	The detection boundary follows a power law in observation length:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \cdot T^{-2.41}.
40	\label{eq:alpha-c}
41	\end{equation}
42	The empirical exponent $\gamma{=}2.41$ admits a first-principles explanation under a thresholded sub-linear signal model.
43	
44	\begin{proposition}[Detection phase boundary]\label{prop:phase-boundary}
45	Under a sub-linear signal model $S(\alpha) = c \cdot \max(0,\, \alpha - \alpha_\infty)^\beta$ with $\beta < 1$, the detection boundary satisfies
46	\begin{equation}
47	\alpha_c(T) = \alpha_\infty + A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)},
48	\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}
49	\end{equation}
50	where $\alpha_\infty$ is the detection floor, $A = (z^* \sigma / c)^{1/\beta}$, and $z^* = \Phi^{-1}(\pi^*)$ \textup{(proof in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof})}.
51	\end{proposition}
52	
53	Fitting to Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$, $\beta{=}0.208$, and $\gamma_{\mathrm{theory}} {=} 1/(2\beta) {=} 2.408$, recovering the empirical $\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\Delta\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$; linear $\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$; Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
54	The floor $\alpha_\infty{=}0.193$ implies contamination below ${\sim}19\%$ produces no causal signal regardless of $T$; at $T{=}5$, $\alpha_c \geq 1.0$.
55	The steepness $k{=}100$ yields a step-like transition ($\alpha_c(10) {=} 0.487$, $\alpha_c(20) {=} 0.248$; \S\ref{sec:regime}, \S\ref{sec:t20}).
56	Signal $S(\alpha)$ rises monotonically, but resonance $R(\alpha)$ drops at $\alpha{\approx}0.60$ when canary and downstream responses compress into a single generation, yielding four regimes: \emph{blind} ($S$ below floor), \emph{onset} ($S$ crosses threshold), \emph{synchronous dip} ($R \to 0$), and \emph{detection} ($S$ dominates).
57	
```

first-differenced distinct-1 $\\varphi{<}0.44$), and 0/24 null rejections confirm adequate size control (Appendix~\\ref{app:nonstationarity}).\nThe same simulation shows PermG retains ${\\sim}80\\%$ of raw-levels power at $T{=}11$ versus ${\\sim}61\\%$ for standard first-differencing, justifying the permutation approach at short series lengths.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nWe model the PermG detection rate as a $T$-dependent step function, excluding the synchronous dip at $\\alpha \\in [0.55, 0.65]$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{rate}(\\alpha, T) = \\frac{0.70}{1 + \\exp\\!\\bigl(-100\\,(\\alpha - \\alpha_c(T))\\bigr)},\n\\label{eq:joint-sigmoid}\n\\end{equation}\nfitted jointly to $T{=}10$ ($n{=}17$) and $T{=}20$ ($n{=}5$) data ($R^2{=}0.883$).\nThe detection boundary follows a power law in observation length:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = 0.193 + 75.68 \\cdot T^{-2.41}.\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\nThe empirical exponent $\\gamma{=}2.41$ admits a first-principles explanation under a thresholded sub-linear signal model.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Detection phase boundary]\\label{prop:phase-boundary}\nUnder a sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\max(0,\\, \\alpha - \\alpha_\\infty)^\\beta$ with $\\beta < 1$, the detection boundary satisfies\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = \\alpha_\\infty + A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\alpha_\\infty$ is the detection floor, $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$, and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ \\textup{(proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof})}.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nFitting to Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c} yields $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$, $\\beta{=}0.208$, and $\\gamma_{\\mathrm{theory}} {=} 1/(2\\beta) {=} 2.408$, recovering the empirical $\\gamma{=}2.41$ ($\\Delta\\gamma{=}0.002$, $R^2{=}1.000$; linear $\\beta{=}1$ rejected, $R^2{=}0.711$; Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nThe floor $\\alpha_\\infty{=}0.193$ implies contamination below ${\\sim}19\\%$ produces no causal signal regardless of $T$; at $T{=}5$, $\\alpha_c \\geq 1.0$.\nThe steepness $k{=}100$ yields a step-like transition ($\\alpha_c(10) {=} 0.487$, $\\alpha_c(20) {=} 0.248$; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}, \\S\\ref{sec:t20}).\nSignal $S(\\alpha)$ rises monotonically, but resonance $R(\\alpha)$ drops at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.60$ when canary and downstream responses compress into a single generation, yielding four regimes: \\emph{blind} ($S$ below floor), \\emph{onset} ($S$ crosses threshold), \\emph{synchronous dip} ($R \\to 0$), and \\emph{detection} ($S$ dominates).\n",

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study lead-lag analysis pitfalls in iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M \cite{biderman2023pythia}, a 410M-parameter decoder-only language model.
8	Pythia provides a controlled setting: small enough for multi-seed experiments across thirteen contamination levels, yet large enough to exhibit meaningful generation-over-generation degradation.
9	Cross-architecture validation uses GPT-2-medium (345M parameters, also decoder-only), a second transformer family of similar scale; results are reported in \S\ref{sec:regime} and \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
10	Training data is drawn from WikiText-103 \cite{merity2017pointer}, using 5{,}000-sample subsets per generation.
11	
12	Our protocol follows the replace paradigm of \citet{shumailov2024curse}, adapted for mixed synthetic-real training at $\alpha{<}1.0$.
13	Each generation $t$ proceeds in three steps: (1)~fine-tune the generation-$(t{-}1)$ model for 5{,}000 steps on a mixture of $\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\alpha)$ fraction real data (AdamW optimizer, learning rate $5 \times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium); (2)~generate 5{,}000 synthetic samples from the fine-tuned model (temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens); (3)~fully replace the synthetic pool.
14	We run $T{=}10$ generations across a 13-point $\alpha$ grid ($\{0.00, 0.05, 0.10, \ldots, 1.00\}$) with multi-seed replication (4 seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$; 3 at primary levels; 1--2 at probing points).
15	
16	We track two \emph{canary metrics}---token entropy ($H(p_\theta | \text{context})$, 1{,}000 held-out samples) and ECE (15 bins)---and three \emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1 and distinct-2~\cite{li2016diversity} and perplexity (evaluated on held-out real data).
17	Additional metrics (distinct-3, effective rank, Self-BLEU) appear in the Appendix.
18	Code and data will be released upon publication.
19	
20	All hypothesis tests apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$.
21	Permutation Granger causality tests use 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \cite{shojaie2022granger}.
22	Cross-correlation analysis uses a maximum lag of 5 generations.
23	Raw metric drift from generation~0 to generation~10 is summarized in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:drift_summary}.
24	
25	
26	\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}
27	\label{sec:regime}
28	
29	Diversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$ (3-seed mean).
30	Despite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger-causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent.
31	The onset we report reflects PermG's statistical power curve crossing the detection floor at a critical contamination level, not the emergence of a new physical signal; this interpretation is confirmed by sub-threshold $p$-values at $\alpha{=}0.45$ (\S\ref{sec:theory}).
32	Permutation Granger (PermG) tests find 0/8 canary$\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jump to 6/8 at $\alpha{=}0.50$ (2~fp32 seeds, consistent).
33	This transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary: $C = 1{-}(1{-}\alpha)^{10}$ yields $99.75\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.45$ versus $99.90\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.50$, a difference of 0.15 percentage points, yet detection jumps from null to reliable.
34	Sigmoid regression on $T{=}10$ data alone yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$); steepness $k$ is not statistically constrainable ($\mathrm{SE}(k) \approx k$), consistent with a step-function transition (Figure~\ref{fig:regime_map}).
35	This single-$T$ estimate is consistent with the joint model (\S\ref{sec:theory}), which yields $\alpha_c(T{=}10) = 0.487$ and predicts how the detection boundary shifts with observation length.
36	We cannot distinguish a sharp phase transition from the inflection point of a smooth power curve at current sample sizes ($T \leq 20$); the detection onset may simply reflect where signal strength crosses the PermG power threshold.
37	A Fisher exact test corroborates the categorical jump: 0/64 pairs FDR-significant at $\alpha \leq 0.45$ versus 18/32 at $\alpha \geq 0.50$ ($p = 3.45 \times 10^{-11}$); sub-threshold $p$-values at $\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) are consistent with proximity to the power boundary (\S\ref{sec:theory}).
38	A systematic robustness analysis (1{,}160 configurations) confirms specification-invariance of the onset.
39	
40	First-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.
41	Canary$\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$, 6 pairs per seed), while canary$\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \approx 0$, $p = 0.99$): the apparent perplexity association is a shared-trend artifact.
42	All canary claims are therefore scoped to diversity metrics only.
43	This scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference, not selective power loss from differencing: $|r|$ retention is ${\geq}95\%$ for both channels, but canary$\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$) while canary$\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\ref{app:channel_effect_size}; perplexity $n{=}8$, limited sample).
44	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed $\times$ 4~seeds; mauve excluded) reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; directional asymmetry is robust across all WikiText conditions.
45	Independent of the Granger test, the degradation cascade confirms temporal precedence: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ onset at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Kendall $\tau{=}0.337$; \S\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\ref{app:moment_cascade}).
46	The canary mechanism also exhibits a blind spot: at $\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\%$ versus $-4.6{\pm}0.4\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.0$).
47	The gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\alpha$ range: $0.5\sigma$ at $\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\sigma$ at $\alpha{=}0.75$ (relative to null variability), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\footnote{Seed~43 at $\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 PermG FDR-significant pairs versus 0/8 for seed~42, consistent with stochastic variation at low signal rather than robust detection; no other below-threshold $\alpha$ level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}
48	Below $\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-causal signal in the canary metrics.
49	
50	\begin{figure*}[t]
51	\centering
52	\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}
53	\caption{Metric trajectories at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead across all four seeds (shaded: inter-seed range). This pattern corresponds to 24/24 forward and 0/24 reverse FDR-significant canary$\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed, mauve excluded).}
54	\label{fig:metric_trajectory}
55	\end{figure*}
56	
57	
58	\subsection{Dose-Response and the $S \times R$ Lens}
59	\label{sec:dose_response}
60	
61	The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:regime_map}) reveals four detection regimes: \emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8 FDR-significant pairs), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), \emph{synchronous dip} ($\alpha \approx 0.60$, 0/8 both seeds; dual-seed confirmed), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{=}0.75$, 0--6/8 per seed, 2 of 3 seeds ${\geq}5/8$; $\alpha{=}1.0$, 6/8 across 4~seeds).
62	The non-monotonic dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ is consistent with the $S \times R$ lens (\S\ref{sec:theory}): $S(\alpha)$ rises monotonically but $R(\alpha)$ drops as relaxation time matches the observation interval, driving $S \times R$ to a local minimum.
63	At $\alpha{=}0.55$, PermG returns 0/8 despite marginal $p$-values ($p_{\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.062$--$0.092$); DiffXCorr detects 8/8 bidirectionally, confirming signal below PermG's directional threshold.
64	
65	Method choice is itself a major source of analytic variability: at $\alpha{=}1.0$, direction-checked forward significance rates span 0\% to 75\% (Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison}), with inter-method agreement at $\kappa{=}0.185$ (near chance); PermG is the only method combining zero null-control FPR with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$.
66	Under $K{\geq}3$ consensus, one false positive appears in seed~44 (te$\to$mauve, 3/5 methods), yielding FPR${=}1/24$ (4.2\%); PermG alone maintains zero empirical FPR.\footnote{NaiveXCorr achieves 100\% null-control FPR and is excluded from the specification curve analysis; results including NaiveXCorr appear in Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}.}
67	A specification curve across 18 method variants confirms the onset: median detection rate jumps from 19\% ($\alpha{=}0.48$) to 69\% ($\alpha{=}0.50$), then drops to 6\% at the synchronous dip ($\alpha{=}0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}).
68	Task degradation corroborates the boundary: perplexity jumps from 31.3 to 41.1 ($+31\%$) between $\alpha{=}0.50$ and $0.52$, aligning with the PermG onset.
69	At very low contamination ($\alpha{=}0.05$), task performance marginally improves over the uncontaminated baseline (perplexity 20.8 vs.\ 26.6), consistent with a data augmentation effect that vanishes at higher mixing ratios (Appendix~\ref{app:task_degradation}).
70	
71

Each cell: fraction of 8 validated pairs at BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$). PermG is the only method combining zero null-control FPR with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$.}

92	
93	
94	\subsection{Extended Observation ($T{=}20$)}
95	\label{sec:t20}
96	
97	$T{=}10$ establishes methodology under limited data; $T{=}20$ provides validation with adequate statistical power.
98	Post-hoc power analysis (simulation under AR(1) DGP; Appendix~\ref{app:nonstationarity}) estimates PermG power at ${\sim}37\%$ for the diversity channel ($\hat{\beta}{\approx}0.59$) and ${\sim}25\%$ for perplexity ($\hat{\beta}{\approx}0.46$) at $T{=}10$; the 6/8 detection rate is consistent with moderate power rather than ceiling performance.
99	At $T{=}20$, estimated power increases to 55--65\% (diversity) and 35--45\% (perplexity), explaining the blind-zone shrinkage at $\alpha{=}0.45$ ($0/8{\to}5/8$).
100	Differenced perplexity channel power is ${\sim}14\%$ ($T{=}11$, $\hat{\beta}{=}0.46$), consistent with systematic non-detection and confirming this reflects low power rather than method failure.
101	We locked predictions at $T{=}10$ before running $T{=}20$ experiments (commit \texttt{bad72cc}), enabling predict-then-verify testing across five pre-registered $\alpha$ levels.
102	Three of five pre-registered predictions matched exactly ($\alpha \in \{0.50, 0.75, 1.0\}$: $6/8{\to}6/8$); a fourth ($\alpha{=}0.60$) matched under FDR correction applied before unblinding but not pre-committed (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}).\footnote{The committed prediction (v1) used raw $p$-values, predicting 3/8 for $\alpha{=}0.60$; a revised file (v2) with BH--FDR correction predicted 0/8, matching the observed 0/8. V2 was created 44\,min before formal analysis but was not git-committed.}
103	The fifth ($\alpha{=}0.45$: $0/8{\to}5/8$) falsified the ceiling model, confirming the blind zone is power-limited; the synchronous dip at $\alpha{=}0.60$ persists ($0/8$; FDR $p{=}0.064$), confirming a structural regime.
104	Additionally, $\alpha{=}0.25$ (included as a negative control) unexpectedly yielded 3/8 detections; the joint model predicts $\alpha_c(T{=}20) = 0.248$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c}), placing $\alpha{=}0.25$ just above the predicted boundary and $\alpha{=}0.45$ (5/8) well within the detection zone.
105	Transfer entropy provides independent validation: forward-dominant TE fraction increases monotonically with $\alpha$ (4/8 pairs at $\alpha{=}0.25$, 5/8 at $0.50$, 8/8 at $1.0$; mean net TE: $0.14 \to 1.59$), confirming the canary$\to$downstream directional structure without relying on the Granger framework (Appendix~\ref{app:te_mi}).
106	Full results appear in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:t20_comparison}.
107

results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:regime} and \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103 \\cite{merity2017pointer}, using 5{,}000-sample subsets per generation.\n\nOur protocol follows the replace paradigm of \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, adapted for mixed synthetic-real training at $\\alpha{<}1.0$.\nEach generation $t$ proceeds in three steps: (1)~fine-tune the generation-$(t{-}1)$ model for 5{,}000 steps on a mixture of $\\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ fraction real data (AdamW optimizer, learning rate $5 \\times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium); (2)~generate 5{,}000 synthetic samples from the fine-tuned model (temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens); (3)~fully replace the synthetic pool.\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations across a 13-point $\\alpha$ grid ($\\{0.00, 0.05, 0.10, \\ldots, 1.00\\}$) with multi-seed replication (4 seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$; 3 at primary levels; 1--2 at probing points).\n\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics}---token entropy ($H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$, 1{,}000 held-out samples) and ECE (15 bins)---and three \\emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1 and distinct-2~\\cite{li2016diversity} and perplexity (evaluated on held-out real data).\nAdditional metrics (distinct-3, effective rank, Self-BLEU) appear in the Appendix.\nCode and data will be released upon publication.\n\nAll hypothesis tests apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$.\nPermutation Granger causality tests use 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\nCross-correlation analysis uses a maximum lag of 5 generations.\nRaw metric drift from generation~0 to generation~10 is summarized in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:drift_summary}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (3-seed mean).\nDespite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger-causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent.\nThe onset we report reflects PermG's statistical power curve crossing the detection floor at a critical contamination level, not the emergence of a new physical signal; this interpretation is confirmed by sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nPermutation Granger (PermG) tests find 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jump to 6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (2~fp32 seeds, consistent).\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary: $C = 1{-}(1{-}\\alpha)^{10}$ yields $99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ versus $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, a difference of 0.15 percentage points, yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSigmoid regression on $T{=}10$ data alone yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$); steepness $k$ is not statistically constrainable ($\\mathrm{SE}(k) \\approx k$), consistent with a step-function transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nThis single-$T$ estimate is consistent with the joint model (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}), which yields $\\alpha_c(T{=}10) = 0.487$ and predicts how the detection boundary shifts with observation length.\nWe cannot distinguish a sharp phase transition from the inflection point of a smooth power curve at current sample sizes ($T \\leq 20$); the detection onset may simply reflect where signal strength crosses the PermG power threshold.\nA Fisher exact test corroborates the categorical jump: 0/64 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha \\leq 0.45$ versus 18/32 at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ ($p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$); sub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) are consistent with proximity to the power boundary (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nA systematic robustness analysis (1{,}160 configurations) confirms specification-invariance of the onset.\n\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 6 pairs per seed), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$): the apparent perplexity association is a shared-trend artifact.\nAll canary claims are therefore scoped to diversity metrics only.\nThis scoping reflects a genuine temporal structure difference, not selective power loss from differencing: $|r|$ retention is ${\\geq}95\\%$ for both channels, but canary$\\to$diversity correlations peak at lagged offsets (81\\% at lag${<}0$, median${=}-1$) while canary$\\to$perplexity correlations are contemporaneous (50\\% at lag${=}0$), producing $20$--$28{\\times}$ lower Granger $F$ for perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:channel_effect_size}; perplexity $n{=}8$, limited sample).\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed $\\times$ 4~seeds; mauve excluded) reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; directional asymmetry is robust across all WikiText conditions.\nIndependent of the Granger test, the degradation cascade confirms temporal precedence: entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ onset at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Kendall $\\tau{=}0.337$; \\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\nThe canary mechanism also exhibits a blind spot: at $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null ($-5.3\\%$ versus $-4.6{\\pm}0.4\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$).\nThe gen-1 entropy deviation spans three orders of magnitude across the $\\alpha$ range: $0.5\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ to $60\\sigma$ at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (relative to null variability), explaining why the blind zone persists despite increasing contamination.\\footnote{Seed~43 at $\\alpha{=}0.10$ shows 3/8 PermG FDR-significant pairs versus 0/8 for seed~42, consistent with stochastic variation at low signal rather than robust detection; no other below-threshold $\\alpha$ level exhibits seed-dependent detection.}\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-causal signal in the canary metrics.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.9\\textwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead across all four seeds (shaded: inter-seed range). This pattern corresponds to 24/24 forward and 0/24 reverse FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs (6 per seed, mauve excluded).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and the $S \\times R$ Lens}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}) reveals four detection regimes: \\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8 FDR-significant pairs), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), \\emph{synchronous dip} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.60$, 0/8 both seeds; dual-seed confirmed), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, 0--6/8 per seed, 2 of 3 seeds ${\\geq}5/8$; $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 6/8 across 4~seeds).\nThe non-monotonic dip at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ is consistent with the $S \\times R$ lens (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}): $S(\\alpha)$ rises monotonically but $R(\\alpha)$ drops as relaxation time matches the observation interval, driving $S \\times R$ to a local minimum.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.55$, PermG returns 0/8 despite marginal $p$-values ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}}{=}0.062$--$0.092$); DiffXCorr detects 8/8 bidirectionally, confirming signal below PermG's directional threshold.\n\nMethod choice is itself a major source of analytic variability: at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, direction-checked forward significance rates span 0\\% to 75\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), with inter-method agreement at $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (near chance); PermG is the only method combining zero null-control FPR with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$.\nUnder $K{\\geq}3$ consensus, one false positive appears in seed~44 (te$\\to$mauve, 3/5 methods), yielding FPR${=}1/24$ (4.2\\%); PermG alone maintains zero empirical FPR.\\footnote{NaiveXCorr achieves 100\\% null-control FPR and is excluded from the specification curve analysis; results including NaiveXCorr appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}.}\nA specification curve across 18 method variants confirms the onset: median detection rate jumps from 19\\% ($\\alpha{=}0.48$) to 69\\% ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), then drops to 6\\% at the synchronous dip ($\\alpha{=}0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}).\nTask degradation corroborates the boundary: perplexity jumps from 31.3 to 41.1 ($+31\\%$) between $\\alpha{=}0.50$ and $0.52$, aligning with the PermG onset.\nAt very low contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.05$), task performance marginally improves over the uncontaminated baseline (perplexity 20.8 vs.\\ 26.6), consistent with a data augmentation effect that vanishes at higher mixing ratios (Appendix~\\ref{app:task_degradation}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Forward-direction FDR significance rates (\\%) across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, fp32; $3\\sigma$ threshold calibration, lag-direction filtering for cross-correlation methods). Each cell: fraction of 8 validated pairs at BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$). PermG is the only method combining zero null-control FPR with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr          & 75   & 62.5 & 62.5 & 0    & 0    & 75  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr         & 0    & 25   & 12.5 & 0    & 0    & 0   \\\\\nThreshold ($3\\sigma$) & 37.5 & 62.5 & 100  & 100  & 25   & 37.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger       & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{75}   & \\textbf{0}   \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto$^\\S$  & 12.5 & 37.5 & 50   & 100  & 12.5 & 12.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\S$TY directional assignment under verification; row retained for completeness.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Pooled FPR (3 seeds, cluster-bootstrap 95\\% CI): \\S\\ref{sec:mmct}.} \\\\\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Extended Observation ($T{=}20$)}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\n$T{=}10$ establishes methodology under limited data; $T{=}20$ provides validation with adequate statistical power.\nPost-hoc power analysis (simulation under AR(1) DGP; Appendix~\\ref{app:nonstationarity}) estimates PermG power at ${\\sim}37\\%$ for the diversity channel ($\\hat{\\beta}{\\approx}0.59$) and ${\\sim}25\\%$ for perplexity ($\\hat{\\beta}{\\approx}0.46$) at $T{=}10$; the 6/8 detection rate is consistent with moderate power rather than ceiling performance.\nAt $T{=}20$, estimated power increases to 55--65\\% (diversity) and 35--45\\% (perplexity), explaining the blind-zone shrinkage at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($0/8{\\to}5/8$).\nDifferenced perplexity channel power is ${\\sim}14\\%$ ($T{=}11$, $\\hat{\\beta}{=}0.46$), consistent with systematic non-detection and confirming this reflects low power rather than method failure.\nWe locked predictions at $T{=}10$ before running $T{=}20$ experiments (commit \\texttt{bad72cc}), enabling predict-then-verify testing across five pre-registered $\\alpha$ levels.\nThree of five pre-registered predictions matched exactly ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.50, 0.75, 1.0\\}$: $6/8{\\to}6/8$); a fourth ($\\alpha{=}0.60$) matched under FDR correction applied before unblinding but not pre-committed (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_prediction_timeline}).\\footnote{The committed prediction (v1) used raw $p$-values, predicting 3/8 for $\\alpha{=}0.60$; a revised file (v2) with BH--FDR correction predicted 0/8, matching the observed 0/8. V2 was created 44\\,min before formal analysis but was not git-committed.}\nThe fifth ($\\alpha{=}0.45$: $0/8{\\to}5/8$) falsified the ceiling model, confirming the blind zone is power-limited; the synchronous dip at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ persists ($0/8$; FDR $p{=}0.064$), confirming a structural regime.\nAdditionally, $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (included as a negative control) unexpectedly yielded 3/8 detections; the joint model predicts $\\alpha_c(T{=}20) = 0.248$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}), placing $\\alpha{=}0.25$ just above the predicted boundary and $\\alpha{=}0.45$ (5/8) well within the detection zone.\nTransfer entropy provides independent validation: forward-dominant TE fraction increases monotonically with $\\alpha$ (4/8 pairs at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, 5/8 at $0.50$, 8/8 at $1.0$; mean net TE: $0.14 \\to 1.59$), confirming the canary$\\to$downstream directional structure without relying on the Granger framework (Appendix~\\ref{app:te_mi}).\nFull results appear in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:t20_comparison}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}",
